ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u1	Good morning. We're going to start the session. The next item is the Official Gazette of Parliament, so we will not proceed to its reading. Mrs Maria Guadalupe Murillo Solis justified her inattentiveness and delegated the vote to Mr Pedro Puy Fraga. The Bureau, now meeting, authorized this delegation to vote. No more, we're on the agenda and we're starting with the legislative texts. Comprehensive discussion of the Bill of Museums and other Museum Centres of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u2	The Socialist Parliamentary Group and the Galician Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group have tabled amendments to this bill. For the presentation of the bill, the Councillor for Culture, Education and University, Mr. Román Rodríguez González has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u3	Thank you very much, President. Ladies and gentlemen, today we bring to this House the draft law of museums and other museums in Galicia, which is the first legal text that will regulate, autonomously and independently, this area of action and which will grant these institutions all the protagonistism and relevance they deserve. This new rule, which begins the parliamentary process today, comes to an ambitious regulatory process—we could define it as a true virtuous circle—by what we have already approved the Library and File and Document Law, and which we hope to be able to culminate next year 2021 with this law, which would be the first Welsh museum law. Access to culture is a right of all people, so it is recognized by our Constitution and also by our Statute of Self-Government. And museums are a fundamental instrument, the most fundamental of which we have the public powers to guarantee this right. They are true institutions serving citizenship and the highest relevance in a democratic and modern society like ours. Galicia currently has 94 museums of very different typology and nature. Our community has experienced an important growth in recent years. Since 1986, when the first regulations appear, the number of museums has increased by 200%. Before the current health context, data indicated that more than one and a half million people were approaching our museums annually. They are also genuine economic assets that depend on a large number of workers, both directly and indirectly. This clear evolution has also highlighted the importance of regulating this area through a comprehensive and specific policy framework. This bill comes to fill an existing void so far. Without a doubt, it will be able to deepen the consolidation and empowerment of the museums in Galicia by fulfilling the constitutional mandates and by being in line with the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. Ladies and gentlemen, we are faced with a standard of technical marking that has a common structure of very diverse institutions. The future law orders all and every one of the typologies that are under the generic concept of "museistic center": the museums themselves, the museum collections and the cultural heritage interpretation centers, which are included for the first time in our legal order. The text defines and limits its functions with a contemporary, plural and integrative perspective; a perspective in which, expressly, the perspective of gender and collectives with greater difficulties of inclusion is taken into account. It addresses aspects related to the organization and comes into regular questions relating to funds, such as allocation, deposits or basic conditions in which conservation or restoration acts must be carried out. This project also collects the necessary conditions for defining, creating and improving museums. It is worth noting that, within these dynamization measures, it focuses on the importance of social participation and private initiative, since museums are opportunities for dialogue with citizens and, therefore, this law believes that they must be empowered to maintain constant dialogue with the public to which they are addressed. Similarly, it takes into account the momentum and the promotion of scientific research, another area that cannot be absent or hidden, since in these centers there is an important study work that facilitates a better understanding of our past and our present. In direct connection, this new legal framework does not forget the pedagogical dimension of these institutions as broadcast and knowledge dissemination agents. There is, of course, also the economic dimension, as museums are important assets capable of boosting the tourist plan, as evidenced by the data we have just seen. In short, the project we have come to present today includes the plural role of museums, a role that is currently not limited to the mere conservation and exhibition of cultural heritage, but takes into account areas such as social, educational, research and economic. Ladies and gentlemen, in addition to establishing the legal regime for all museums, this law creates, as a great novelty, the Greek System of Museum Centers; an organizational and functional structure that, with a wide dimension, will allow to efficiently coordinate the cultural resources of the centres that integrate it, and that will, of course, respect its autonomy at all times. This system will consist of an administrative body, the Culture Council itself, which will have leadership and coordination functions and will design and plan the museum policy in Galicia. Secondly, it creates the Council of Museum Centers of Galicia, a council for cooperation between the different entities that provide services in our community. The system will also include the different museums, museum collections and heritage interpretation centers in Galicia. Belonging to this structure will bring together a number of benefits, such as participation in aid lines, priority in access to certain collaborative or formative actions, or participation in cultural and tourist promotion initiatives. Different from the system, but as part of it, it is also planned to create networks of museum centers. The aim of this figure will be to promote cooperation and cross-cutting use of resources. Museums will be able to voluntarily adhere to networks, which will be established in the light of different areas, such as title, thematic content or museological discourse. These are operating subsystems that will operate under criteria of efficiency and constant improvement. All these contents and all these news are the fruit of an open process. From the German Government we wanted the drafting of the text of the law to be participatory. That is why we have created a working group consisting of representatives from various areas of the museum sector. In total, we invite about 50 recognized prestigious entities, both public and private, representatives of very different realities. This process and these entities have contributed to the orientation and consensus of the content of the regulation. Through their approach, we were able to carry out a correct diagnosis of the starting situation and first-hand knowledge of the interests and needs of these centres. It was a very participatory process that led to amendments prior to parliamentary procedure being almost non-existent, to a small number of them. The result of all the approaches made will advance in universal accessibility and equality. The new law presents museums as cultural centres within the reach of society and bets on non-discrimination and taking into account the needs of collectives with greater difficulties of inclusion. Throughout the drafting process, we have responded to many of the approaches that have been made by entities such as the Galician Confederation of Persons with Disabilities or bodies such as the Economic and Social Council. In the same way, this project bets on the implementation of new technologies. The goal is twofold: to facilitate citizen access to the museum heritage and to introduce integrated information, documentation and management systems. Ladies and gentlemen, we're making an important leap. With this bill, the German Government once again expresses its commitment to culture, a commitment that not only aims through an undeniable legislative advance in the area of museums, but also in the budget. Until 2022, we will invest 36 million euros to ensure the functioning of these institutions, these appropriations. This investment represents a 12% increase over the previous year. We are already looking at this amount about 10 million for the programming of the multitude of activities that will take place in its midst; and all this at the doors of Xacobeo 2021, which will be a real dynamizer of cultural consumption and where museums will have a relevant role. Finally, the new law of museums and other museums in Galicia comes to respond to the needs and demands of citizenship in its relationship with museums and to fill the existing normative void with an ambitious, specific and broad-eyed framework. For this reason, I also want to appeal to consensus in such a sensitive area as this, the scope of museums. The text that we are debating today in this Parliament is up to the role of these institutions in our time, in ordering and regulating these resources and in providing them with their own identity. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, we have drafted this project, basically technical profile, listening to the sector and the experts, and waiting for the opposition to know how to deal with it in this case. Nothing more and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u4	Thank you, Counselor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u5	The role of defence of amendments to the whole, return and position of the bill. For the Socialist Parliamentary Group, Mrs. Díaz Varela.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u6	Thank you, President. Good morning to all of you. We present this amendment to the whole about the Bill of Museums and other museum centers in Galicia because, although we agree with the need for a law specifically dedicated to museums and this type of centers, this project is not of our liking, since its content does not regulate with the extent and accuracy necessary fundamental aspects of this activity. But well, before we enter the text, we think we should start at the beginning — for a bad start or a bad start — because in March 2019 there was a meeting of the councillor and the Director General of Culture — as the Counsellor has already said — with representatives of 30 Greek institutions — it seems that 50 were invited but only 30 — to advance the process of drafting this law. Until then, nothing to object. At that meeting — especially at work — they heard the first technical approaches by the professionals involved in these museum activities. You said in a press release -- because you were talking about transparency -- that it was about achieving a participatory and transparent process. Of course, we wonder where that transparency is if there isn't — or at least we haven't — any reports or documents on that meeting or about the work of that group. We would like, of course, to have someone explain how many times this group has met, because the fact is that there is no report or conclusions prepared by it or any minutes raised from the sessions, their composition or the reports drawn up... that is, nothing. So it is true that we can question what they say is actually achieved. So, yes, we started wrong and already doubting the imperative necessity of this law. And I say "imperious need" because it seems like it was the way it was sold on its day. But it doesn't seem like it should be so imperious if we don't really know what the opinion of the sector was, of the people involved. By going to the text, from our point of view they are already beginning to go wrong in the main, which is the description of "museum", because for this parliamentary group it is not only an institution dedicated to promotion and cultural development, but its existence is also defined by the dissemination of cultural memory, the promotion of identity and cultural development, yes, but also social development. And they also forget, in our opinion, to clarify what the mission of the museum centers is. They forget that fundamental mission, which is the dissemination of cultural memory. But they don't even define precisely the different types of centers. For example, the article on interpretation centres is totally confusing, when this was one of the basic objectives for which the Board justified this legislative text. It also does not do so with primary aspects, such as museum management, documentary management and the use of information and communication technologies. You just said here that it was one of the important issues, another of the elements that underlined your advice, and we, of course, see that this bill has little to say about ICT. Even less it pushes the promotion of museums to make them real elements of cultural diffusion and social cohesion. But there's more oysters or empty ones. The bill, for example, does not include any obligation or measure on the part of the Galician Union — especially its obligation — for the impulse, encouragement or economic support of museums other than its title or management, nor even for the members of the Galician Museum System. The reality is that it doesn't even cover the obligations of the Autonomic Administration with these centres. It seems that the Galician Union just wanted to add a more legal initiative to the whole of the past legislation. I mean, to advertise your management again. That's what we're talking about perfectly in the press notes, because it's the only thing we know about this process. Those press releases published on this bill, by the way, accumulate a lot of inaccuracies, like, for example, they say, or they say, that this was a pioneering rule in the autonomic field. Most communities in the Spanish state already have similar laws, some elaborated more than 20 years ago. Summer, they have specific laws about museums Castile and León, Castile-A Mancha, Cantabria, Andalusia, Catalonia, Aragon, Euskadi, Madrid, Navarre and Murcia. So we don't understand that as pioneers. Another "information imprecision" of the Galician Union — by taking out a little of an euphemistic term — is given when it tries to explain to the media its projects, and that that "will first legislate the role of museums", when you know that these museums were already regulated in the Law on Cultural Heritage. Despite what is stated in the explanatory statement, this bill, of course, will not provide greater protection to the cultural heritage of what is already developed in that Law 5/2016, of Galicia's cultural heritage, which already regulates museums and museum collections and foresees a regulatory development that essentially contains — although quite confusingly — this bill that we are discussing today. The question is that, although the last third provision of the law in force — since Law 5/2016 — enabled the Council of Galicia for its regulatory development, it was not carried out. But what is also curious is that much of the content collected in the new text, much of the new text, should be the object of regulatory development and not appear here too reflected. I don't know if there's a problem with the regulatory developments that oppose us. In other words, given the content of this text, it would be perfectly useful to reflect in the Law of 4 May of the cultural heritage, in its title VIII, devoted entirely to museums — Articles 111 to 118. You say -- we don't say it -- in the council's own report that joins in this bill: "Title VIII of the Cultural Heritage Act already regulated the so-called visiting collections, while laying the foundations of what is conceived as a network of the Greek Museum System." This bill is not what he talks about for the first time either the German Museum System or the network, but it was already mentioned in that law. You said, "... while laying the foundations of what is conceived as a network of the Welsh Museum System to integrate all those museums and visiting collections that are found in the territory of the autonomous community." But, continuing with Law 5/2016, this text is drawn up with such a lack of attention that it is not coordinated with it in several respects. For example, the archaeological finding deposit scheme is not collected, when in article 99 of the aforementioned law we are talking about casual findings indicating that they can be delivered to a public museum. It is also surprising that references to the museums of the Heritage Law, as if museums were not part of the cultural heritage, given that they are transmitters and depositors of the collective memory, are eliminated in the derogatory provision. Among the shortcomings of this text is especially serious also the lack of references to the planning of museum staff and museum centers and the setting of the functions of each job in the different functional areas that are envisaged. It is also not acceptable for it to be legislated that the leadership functions of museums of ownership or self-management may be assumed by the mid-level scale of museums, when there is a higher scale. It does not seem to be acceptable either to the desire to introduce and control the Council of Galicia in the autonomy of the other public administrations that appears in this bill. This same willingness to dominate the system by the Galician Board was also appreciated in Article 10, which regulates the composition of the Council of Museums of Galicia, where all members are appointed by the head of the council and their election is somewhat arbitrary. And at this last point, we wanted to stop a little bit, because, of course, from our point of view, what is entirely unacceptable is that the Galician Council of Culture has absolutely no presence in this bill. It is not in the relationship of bodies and entities which, according to the Council of Culture, constituted a working group or were invited to draw up this text. Nor is it expected that the Galician Culture Council will be part of the Galician Museum Centres Council. He doesn't show up, he doesn't show up ex-profess. By the way, it is not expected that the municipalities, represented by the Galician Federation of Municipalities, are part of that council, given that many museums in our country are of municipal ownership. But following the Galeg Culture Council, it seems to us to be intolerable, of course, that the Galician Council did not ask it to issue a report on this bill, which, in addition, confirmed its president last week. Mrs Rosario Álvarez confirmed that, indeed, no report had been requested on this project. Of course, since the Galician Council of Culture has as its functions to investigate and evaluate the cultural needs of the people of Galicia by trying to make the most valuable products available to all Galician citizenship, both in their own culture and universal culture, to analyze those issues that refer to the Greek cultural heritage in their various facets and levels, in view of their preservation, their study, their valorization and dissemination, and to advise the public authorities of the autonomous community, especially the Galician Union, we believe that it is the institution that could best inform and correct this bill, max when it has a section of work specializing in heritage and cultural goods. And, of course, he has a lot of experience in studying museums. Something that also surprises us is the elimination of an express reference to continuity in the recognition of the competence of the Galego People's Museum to act as a synthesis center of all museums and collections, both public and private, integrated into the Greek system of museums dedicated to the study, promotion and dissemination of Galician historical and anthropological heritage, as contained in Decree 111/1993 of 22 May of the Council of Culture and Youth. We also know that it was the Museum of the Galician People that he had to request later his inclusion. For all these reasons, of course, we consider this bill unacceptable, which was not born — or does not seem to us to be born — to legislate the centres which should be guaranteed by our collective, cultural and social memory, but it was, as I said, a more propaganda act to put crosses in the excellence of works done, but it does not intend to clarify, manage, organize and protect our museum heritage. For all this, we logically demand the return of this bill. Thank you.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u7	Thank you, Mrs Varela. [[One person from the House's cleaning services comes to disinfect the floor.]] You now have the floor to Mrs. Complaines Zas.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u8	Good morning, gentlemen. Since the Galician Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group we had hoped that, after so many years of promising and advancing this law of museums and other museums in Galicia, the Government of the People's Party would bring us a truly pioneer legal framework, adjusted to the most innovative currents in the science of the museumology. We trusted in their ability to come up with a document that finally established the foundations of a living and truly inclusive museum network. But I must anticipate since reading his preliminary draft law has led to our disappointment that we cannot regard it as a sufficient legal framework and also to the opportunities of our immense museum heritage. That's why we're asking for your return. We do it, to see, for two great reasons of weight, one meets the shape and the other meets its background, which I will begin to explain briefly. As far as form is concerned, it must be pointed out that in democracy the content of legislative projects is as important as the procedure of their processing. In this process, if something has not been lost — or at least so they have come to understand it — it was a lack of time or improvisation that prevented a high level of consultation and specialized contrast that a legal framework of these characteristics needs. That's why it's always unacceptable, strong and inappropriate for a government — when it's not the first time they've tried to legislate to the shortcut — that once again the Galician Board of Directors has made clear fundamental consultative bodies, such as the Galician Council of Culture — of course, referred to in the Budget Project, with which we've been working these days, as the high-level board of the Galician Union, the university or academies, as well as other institutions and entities, to speak through the corresponding reports on this preliminary draft museum law and other museum centres in Galicia, which is a practice that we see to be a good test and guarantee of democratic maturity. It must be noted that Article 32 of the Statute of Self-Government places the Galician Culture Council as the highest body for consulting and consulting the public authorities of the Autonomous Community in the field of culture and heritage. We would like to highlight some justified reason for such absence, but the recent presence of the president of the Galician Culture Council in Commission 3 has ratified the worst agoure: the Galician Council did not request the report from the Galician Council of Culture. We regret this deep disillusionment — when I do not exercise silence — of a high institution with high statutory responsibility. The Galician Council of Culture Act states in its Title II, concerning competences, which are the functions of this statutory institution to consider how many matters refer to the cultural heritage, to promote the language and culture of the Greek people, to investigate and evaluate the cultural needs of the Greek people, to advise and consult the powers of the Autonomous Community where necessary for the defence and promotion of the cultural values of the Greek people and to raise to the powers of the Autonomous Community reports and proposals for the defence and promotion of the cultural values of the Greek people. It is equally surprising that neither one of the other entities mentioned above, such as the Royal Galaga Academy of Fine Arts or the universities, collected in the Galician Cultural Heritage Act — in particular in Article 7 — as advisory and advisory bodies in this area. It should be borne in mind that this law is the direct history of the draft law of museums and other museums in Galicia that the Government now presents to us. None of these institutions and entities also participated in the working group set up by the Council of Culture and Tourism at that time, with which the Council of Galicia spoke in the drafting of the text, according to the information reached in memory on the allegations presented to the draft law. As in so many times, this is again a perverse practice of end-of-life politics. We make that we offer a democratic menu, well-customed of headlines and administrative literature at their point, but then there are no options to choose who to consult, because their kitchen is that of the single command, a sad definition of democracy and child respect for the Greek cultural values. On the other hand, and also in relation to due transparency and participation in the legislative process, it is highly questionable that the public exposure period coincided with the summer months — from July 10, 2019 to August 1, of the same year — which obviously makes it difficult ––we know it well–– – to present the arrivals, and which was evidenced that only three claims were collected, as stated in the above-mentioned memory. As for the background or the concrete content collected in the articulate of this bill, since the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego we want to make the following considerations, which, in our view, support the present amendment of return. First of all, the bill collects a conservative and limited definition and consideration of museums and museums, even outdated, because it does not take into account the recommendations made by the ICOM at the 139th meeting of its board of directors, developed in July 2019 in Paris or the new trends in the thought of museumology – – – by the way, a word that has little impact on this preliminary draft law –, beginning with social museum, aimed at enhancing the social function of museums as tools for the development of communities and social transformation. Even the word "museology" – – I insist –, with all that it means as the science of the management of the museum, is merely reflected in this document; which, in some ways, also expresses the delay in the empowerment of as attractive natural and tourist resource as is the museum and the professionalization of staff: the museumologist or museumologist. In fact, this preliminary draft maintains the definition of "museum" collected in Galician Cultural Heritage Act of 2016, a new proof of the anachronic basis that supports this legislative support. Secondly, it is particularly chaotic that the traces that make up the museums are not included in their own definition in Article 3 and should be expected to arrive at Article 13 — as the Economic and Social Council has also observed in its report. The same is true of museum collections and interpretation centers. Thirdly, although UNESCO's recommendations on the protection and promotion of museums and their collections are cited in the explanatory statement, they are not later taken into account in the articulate. Thus, the bill does not collect the necessary collaboration between museums and civil society — such as development and social transformation tools — nor the adoption of codes of ethics and good practice, nor the mechanisms of cooperation between museums and other institutions related to culture, heritage and education. And it even contradicts the articles of the 2016 Cultural Heritage Act itself, such as, for example, Article 6, which alludes to collaboration with the Catholic Church, who owns — well we know — an important cultural legacy, subject on occasion to rules of closure that hide it and of what actually ignores its museum value. Fourthly, it excludes from this legal document the intangible cultural heritage and industrial heritage — also deep-rooted and encouragement in our culture — as defined in Galicia's Cultural Heritage Act. And the historical memory is also openly excluded. Fifthly, we understand that this is a very ambitious, unclear bill in many respects, even – and it must also be said – disorderly and to some extent chaotic in its formulation – – as the Economic and Social Council also points out in its report – that it violates a basic standard of any legal document and any administrative procedure, as is the importance of the ease of understanding for compliance. Sixthly, this draft museum law does not collect the competences of the Autonomous Community in relation to museums and other museums. Nor does it raise the general criteria that public policies in this area should follow, nor the clarification of those competences and obligations that correspond to other levels of the Administration – – which also have them –, as is the case with the municipalities and provincial deputies, which, moreover, are holders of some of these spaces, or other museum funds belonging to the military heritage, port, etc. To speak, as it is collected in the articulation, coordination and cooperation between administrations without clarifying competences or drawing up formulas to achieve these objectives means only a declaration of intent, but it was necessary to move forward. In seventh place, this bill waives clearly regulating museums or museum collections that are held by institutions such as the Church or the Army, as well as the obligations that these have in order to guarantee – among other issues – the right of access, not only for interested visitors, but also for investigators, and it leaves in their hands the aforementioned conditions beyond the principle of non-discrimination, which shows little ambition. From this legislative framework, it is necessary to open channels for the management of this heritage from optional museum optics, which affect security, conservation, documentation, study, digitisation — ultimately disclosure — which include, in addition, this legislative and practical interaction, the Cultural Heritage Prosecutor's Office and the police specialized in crimes against it, among other departments. In the eighth place, we also understand that it is obvious in this text — even in the exhibition of motives — that the historical tradition of Welshism in museum matter, as well as the national identity of Galicia, when proposing new categories, such as that of "museums of national interest", understood as the one that identifies spaces that exceed its framework and have a special meaning for Galician cultural heritage — something that proves immaturity in a matter that has already been dealt with at the Galego Studies Seminar itself almost a century ago. In this regard, it should be clear that the primary idea of empowerment of the Museum of the Galician People as the National Museum of Galicia is similar to that of Wales, Cardiff, or the National Museum of Scotland. In the ninth place, it could be added to this bill for the empowerment of museum centers, with everything that involves their management, those significant collections of the same administrations, capital councils, deputies, Parliament itself, the board of Galicia, universities, etc., as a way of documenting and disseminating the origins and ends of this heritage. And in the tenth and last place, with regard to the Greek System of Museum Centers — which constitutes the central element of the bill — it should be remembered that it had already appeared previously collected in the Galician Cultural Heritage Act — in article 114 in particular — without having been developed for the last four years since its entry into force. Title II is the most chaotic part of the bill, especially with regard to the management of its content and the rights and obligations of the centres integrated into the Galician System of Museum Centers. In this sense, the text lacks concreteness in fundamental aspects for its application, such as the necessary definition of the content of the museological plan, the annual programming of activities, the management memory, the organisation of staff, as well as the functions of management. With regard to the Council of Museums of Galicia, the bill merely gives representation to the Government of Galicia and to the persons appointed by it, and excludes from other institutions such as the Parliament of Galicia, the Fegamp or, as otherwise, the Council of Galician Culture. Finally, the draft law does not include a specific paragraph aimed at regulating the work of inspection, as a mechanism to ensure strict compliance with the obligations laid down in the standard. As stated above, the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group considers this amendment to be sufficiently justified to the entire Bill of Museums and other Museum Centers of Galicia and calls for the return to the Government. Thank you.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u9	Thank you, Mrs. Complaints. [[One person from the House's cleaning services comes to disinfect the floor.]] For the non-amendant parliamentary group, the People's Parliamentary Group, Mr Ferro Iglesias has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u10	President, Bureau members, ladies and gentlemen, very good morning to all. First of all, I would like to congratulate the Government and, by extension, the Councillor of Culture on this new law, a dialogue and consensus law. We want it to be a law of the future and that's why we're going to take your contributions and partial amendments very seriously. With this law, Galicia will be one of the Spanish autonomys that will have a specific museum law. We won't be the first, right, but we'll try to be the strictest and most effective with this technical, modern law, and, yes, adapted to the present and future reality of our museums. I assure you that we have put on and will continue to put forth every effort to make it a participatory law, because we understand that a law of protection of our culture and our traditions cannot separate us. That's why I know we will overcome political discrepancies and reach many points of encounter. Galicia needed a standard that would regulate the museum sector flexibly and look to the future, that would provide legal security and enhance the activity developed in the museum environment, and that it would take into account the plural dimension of its role within modern and democratic society. As you know, our community has exclusive competence in heritage matters. The first museum regulation is a decree of the year 1986. Later, in 1995 the Galician Heritage Act regulates the area of museum in Title V. This law was also repealed by a new law of 2016, which deals with Title VIII on museums. Now we'll have a specific and own law for the museums. The future law will be the first specific regulation that will regulate this matter in our community and address globally the challenges facing these institutions in the twenty-first century as well as first-level cultural services within the reach of society as a whole, both Greek and foreign. In addition, it incorporates the approaches of different groups and institutions. This is a clearly technical law that is born with the aim of structuring the internal functioning of the ninety-four museum institutions registered in our community, including public and private centres. This is the first law of this issue in our sphere. It is the law that was lacking in the normative articulation of reference cultural institutions — that is, of our archives, libraries and museums —: the Library Law of the year 2012, the Archives and Museums Law of the year 2014 and now, to finish the cycle, the Museum Law. During the period of allegations and reports of the future law, improvements were included aimed at integrating the gender perspective of these appropriations and respect for plurality, participation and collaboration of different social agents, and also making these centres fully accessible. In this way, this regulation meets the considerations expressed by the Galician Confederation of Persons with Disabilities and the Economic and Social Council. [[Cogami]] It presents museums as cultural centres within reach of society, in conditions of equality and non-discrimination, and considers, in addition, the needs of collectives with greater difficulties of social inclusion. Even so, it is apostatized by the implementation of new technologies in these appropriations with a double objective of facilitating citizenship access to the museum heritage but also in order to introduce integrated information, documentation and management systems that improve the functioning of these centres. The direct participation of the sector was carried out, as the advisor said, through a working group formed by March 2019. In this working group, ladies and gentlemen, there were members of the Galician Culture Council. This working group has allowed professionals of recognized prestige to participate in the drafting phase of the text and has served to take the momentum of the situation and the interests of the sector in our community, thus achieving a better orientation of its contents. So, the objective of this law is to promote the Greek museums as reference assets in the social, scientific, educational and also economic plans. To this end, the Board — also reminded the Councillor — will invest EUR 36 million up to 2022 to ensure the operation of these appropriations. Within that amount, some 10 million will be included for the programming of activities of the Greek museums. The future law has as its object the regulation of the legal regime of museums, museum collections and, as you know, for the first time, of the centres of interpretation of Galicia's cultural heritage, as well as the establishment of a Greek System of Museum Centres to improve organizationally and functionally the centres that will integrate the said system. This system will be structured around an administrative body of leadership and coordination, a consultative body called the Council of Museum Centers of Galicia, and includes all museums, museum collections and interpretation centres that integrate into this system. The project defines each of the typologies that integrate the broader and generic concept of "museistic center", and defines the conditions necessary for its creation, as well as organizational and management aspects. In addition, it regulates a whole series of dynamizing measures in the museum area and finally describes and also sets the applicable sanctioning regime. The basic organization of museums is determined, which will include the existence of a governing body and the following basic functional areas, which will develop their activities through the necessary internal coordination. I mean the area of documentation, conservation and research, the area of dissemination and communication, and finally the area of administration. With regard to sponsorship, it is stated that the Autonomic Administration will actively promote sponsorship actions directed at the support of museum centres, as well as the development of its projects. With regard to social participation, public administrations will encourage the creation and promotion of the work of associations of friends from museums and other Greek museums in order to enhance and disseminate their role in society and facilitate their participation in these institutions. A regulation is made according to the role of museums in our time by expressly integrating their social, pedagogical, research and economic function. And I'm done. Finally, this bill, which expresses the commitment of the Greek public authorities to the fulfilment of constitutional mandates and the achievement of a system of museum centres specific to our autonomous community, presents the museum centres with the contemporary perspective that existes the present times: centres with a broad, plural and inclusive function within a society also plural and democratic; centres that, beyond the classical cultural aspect, are powerful economic and social assets. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u17	Thank you, Mrs. Complaints. [[One person from the House's cleaning services comes to disinfect the floor.]] [[Pause.]] We continue the debate on legislative texts. Debates of the entire Galician Contaminated Waste and Soil Project
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u18	Amendments have been tabled to the entire Socialist Parliamentary Group and to the Parliamentary Group of the Galician Nationalist Block. For the introduction of the bill, Mrs. Ánggeles Vázquez Mejuto has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u19	Thank you, President. Good morning. Ladies and gentlemen, let us bring to this House today the project of what will be the future Waste and Contaminated Lands Act of Galicia, a text that is supposed to fulfill our commitment to Galicia, to the Green Galicia, to the future Galicia, to the sustainable Galicia, to the resilient Galicia, to the Galicia that fights climate change. So we started updating and adapting the law after twelve years in which everything changed, both how to consume and how to generate waste and how to manage it. What we want is to provide Galicia with a new legal framework adapted to Community legislation, which is complete, clear and legally safe. We are aware of the need to adapt legislation to the changing model that is being experimented, a system that walks towards a circular economy and is called to be the only model of the future possible in Galicia. In fact, in the last decade Galicia has always been close to and in the vanguard of European waste planning. Thus, in 2010 we designed the Galician Urban Waste Management Plan, which allowed our community to be able to move towards a new design of waste policies. In 2016 we approved the Industrial Waste Plan of Galicia, the PRIGA. He will be alive until 2022 and will allow them to assess how these wastes are being managed and whether his generation is becoming independent of economic growth. I have to inform you that you will soon see the first PRIGA report, which analyses from 2016 to 2018 — the latest data we have on 2018 — and in which we have authorized a total of 750 plants. From 2016 to 2018 industrial waste decreased by 8.5%. We are destined for recycling and recovery of a total of 82% of Galicia's industrial waste, and its disposal, 18%. This means that we not only plan the results, but we are aware of the results, which are the ones that are now transcending and that we will soon introduce: in 2016, 3 million tons of industrial waste; in 2018, 2.8 million tons — of which recycling is destined to 82% —; in 2019 we have two milestones, two important keys: the first is that we gave green light to the Strategy of Climate Change and Energy 2050 and the second is that we approve the Welsh Strategy of the circular economy 2030, with the objective of moving from a linear economy to a circular economy — in which the lines of waste policies are defined in the most relevant sectors and all its inspiring principles have been added to this bill. Now we want to move ahead firmly in a modern and effective waste management. That's why we are faced with a very necessary law for our autonomous community, with which we advance the Central Government's approval of state legislation, which had to be already operational in July 2020 and which today has only one joint text. So we're closing, from Galicia, the circle of necessary tools and putting ourselves at the head of Europe in a circular economy. In short, this is a law that stands out for being more complete and technically appropriate to give real and necessary answers to the activity in Galicia. We simplify the administrative burden, which is fundamental, we improve control but also manage administrative procedures and bet on green employment. One of the guiding principles of the law is hierarchy in waste management, and it focuses especially on the first step, which is fundamental: the prevention of these wastes. The law does not only make European and national objectives its own, but in some cases, such as the reduction of waste we produce, we advance ourselves in five years — from 2030 to 2025 — with a view to reducing them by 15% on the data we have of the year 2010. In addition, in Galicia we mark ourselves as a target to achieve in 2021 that more than 50% of textile waste, as well as electrical appliances and furniture, are introduced into the repair chain for reuse — a percentage that will be 55% for the year 2025 and 60% for the year 2030. In this same line, the text also includes the establishment of centres specialized in the repair of waste reuse, especially waste from electrical, electronic, textile and furniture appliances. In fact, we've already had some program, some pilot plan, like what we inaugurated in the year 2019 in Valga Town Hall. The new text envisages the progressive increase in the re-use or material recovery of non-hazardous wastes of construction as well as demolition, up to 75% in 2025 — compared to 70% proposed by the State. It also sets recycling targets by boosting the recycling of single-use packaging, with the goal of being able in the year 2025 to recycle up to 65% of these, and reaching 70% on the horizon of the year 2030. As regards the shipment of waste to landfills — as well as another European target — it states that only 10% of the waste generated to landfills can be shipped. That's what we call "zero technical truth." With regard to this point, I would like to remind you that since 2009 the German Government has put an end to the landfills and we are moving towards a proper management of our waste. We ended up with the dump model that meant depositing the garbage directly on the territory and without control and security measures. And in 2013 we closed the last unauthorized municipal dump existing in Galicia. Even so, since April 2009 we have eliminated more than a thousand points of uncontrolled dumping throughout Galicia. Between this year and last year, it was more than 200 of the spills that we eliminated. The future law also lays the groundwork for plants to reach this technical vertigo in 2035; that is to say, lead to the single and exclusively waste dump that we are not able to deal with on any of the pathways, a goal that Sogama is about to fulfill this same year. That means we're moving ahead with the European guidelines for fifteen years. I'll give you an example. The direct entry of waste into the Areosa landfill — which is Sogama’s — dropped from 209,000 tons in 2018 to 40,679 tons in 2019 —a transcendental step towards that goal. The future waste law has as one of its basic pillars collaboration, support and coordination with the municipalities, as it could not be otherwise. We recognize their potential, and we also know their problem. But, on the other hand, we are aware of their abilities so that we all engage in a common objective and therefore that the data we draw to public light are met everywhere. One of the main points of collaboration of the Union is the local authorities, which will continue to do, as is their obligation, the management of bioresidues, as municipalities must establish separate collection from the year 2023. They know that the articulated text of the Central Government here makes a separation between municipalities of less than 5,000 inhabitants and more than 5,000 inhabitants, so that the municipalities of more than 5,000 inhabitants begin their management from the year 2021. Galicia opts for all the municipalities, both of the least and of the most inhabitants, to make this collection separate from the year 2023. We are aware of this goal, so in the budgets of the year 2021 we reserve a total of 25 million euros for the construction of three bioresidue plants. The first will be in Vilanova de Arousa and will serve a total of 25 municipalities. It is, of course, added to the opening during this year established in the municipality of Cerceda. In fact, last week we auctioned the project of Vilanova de Arousa. It's a basic project. It will be a pioneering and modern plant in the use of efficient technologies, and it will facilitate the creation of 30 jobs, between direct and indirect. With regard to the municipal area, it should be recalled that the municipalities should also implement the collection of textile and hazardous waste from domestic origin one year later — before December 31, 2024 —. Another of the basic objectives of this text is to drastically reduce the consumption of plastics from one use. Human action is plasticizing the planet, particularly the marine environment, so we have to get the life of plastics to circulate. That's why the law prohibits the free delivery of plastic bags and the sale of single-use plastic vases. And they do it for two main reasons: they are products that demand a lot of energy for an excessively short useful period and, when they come into contact with the ecosystem -- in the seas, for example -- they produce damage and consequences that are devastating. We must remember that 80% of marine waste comes from the land and that one of the most abundant waste in the seas is plastic. That's why we must fight to avoid the plasticization of the planet and, above all, the plasticification of our seas. Braking food waste is another of the evasive measures that the German Government wants to respond to the entry into force of this new normative text. In this sense, I would like to remind you that about a third of the world's foods are lost or wasted, which amounts to 1,300 million tons a year. We must reduce food loss, not only for food, but also for saving on resources, both those needed to produce them and those needed to manage them later as waste. Besides, as citizens we must be more responsible for our buying habits. To be more attentive to what we should discard or to what we should take advantage of, as 42% of food waste is generated in our homes. To achieve this food waste reduction target, the law states that in 2025 we have to reduce by 30% the amount of food waste that we generate over the year 2010, a percentage that takes 50% five years later, in the year 2030. In order to achieve this, catering holders will be obliged to deliver, if the customer so requests, the spare portions of food not consumed on their own premises. And they should do it in compostable containers at 50%, with biodegradable material, never in single-use plastic materials. In addition, we will promote a code of good practice in relation to food surpluses; that is, a social pact, involving all the agents involved in the Horeca channel, to mitigate the production of food waste. So, and for all this, a lot of training and a lot of environmental education is needed, because in this way we will enable the consumption of raw materials and products to take place from an increasingly responsible perspective. In fact, this law bets on pushing younger people to act as catalysts for a better future. It's going to be their future, what belongs to them, but also what they deserve. Finally, the text also makes a special bet on a sustainable and environmentally friendly administration. For this purpose, it includes numerous measures, including the use of renewable energy in the administration and the impulse of the so-called 'green public contract'. But this law not only deals with waste and its management, it focuses on the prevention of contaminated soils. It is established that soils must retain their natural functions at its maximum level, and recover using the best available techniques and always prioritising on-site recovery treatments. What we want is to promote better recognition and control of the quality of our soils. To achieve this, the creation of a complete inventory of soils will be established through the Sun Quality Register. This is complemented by a sanctioning regime that defines new sanctions for contaminated soils, with a maximum amount that can reach 1.7 million euros in the case of very serious sanctions. Finally, we are faced with an essential law for Galicia that will allow us to continue working towards a more sustainable future. It is a text with which Galicia advances to state legislation, which currently simply consists of an articulated text which has not yet obtained the visa and pleasure, or the green light, of the Council of Ministers to be referred to Congress. Suppose a bet determined by the transformation of Galician society into a committed model of different production and consumption, and that seeks to achieve a resilient Galicia that works to reduce climate change...
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u20	Finish, please.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u21	...and to gather the efforts of the Government of Greece to meet the sustainable development goals in which the 2030 Agenda is being implemented. It is therefore a law that responds to environmental, social and economic challenges, and that will enable Galicia to become a community adapted to the changes and challenges facing us in the future. Nothing more and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u22	Thank you, Counselor. [[One person from the House's cleaning services comes to disinfect the floor.]] The role of defence of amendments to the whole and position of the bill. For the Socialist Parliamentary Group, you have the floor to Mrs. Castro Rey.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u23	Thank you, President. From the Socialist Parliamentary Group we are considering the return of this text from Galicia's Bill of Waste and Contaminated Lands because, although we are defending the need for a legal amendment to the Waste Act of 2008, which is currently in force, we believe that this new text should be much more ambitious. This text is in the middle of what we had and the progress in European postulates in dealing with waste management, as I shall develop below. Without going into assessing the point changes of its articulation — because this is not the moment — the fundamental and weightier reasons for proposing our refund amendment are, on the one hand, the practical lack of regulation of plastics — because they must be integrated here and should not be left for specific regulation because they are present in most of the products, and on the other hand, the difference in the concept of waste management, since from the Government of the Union we continue to tend to centralized management and we advocate more for proximity management. For us Socialists, the first objective of any waste policy must be to minimize the negative effects of waste generation, both on human health and on the environment. Even so, and in line with the principles governing the circular economy, we understand that this policy must also have an object, on the one hand, to make efficient use of resources, with a determined strategic commitment from the Government of Galicia, good coordination with the rest of the public bodies — and I refer to the councils and the four deputies — as well as the involvement and commitment of all economic and social agents, and essentially also the involvement of citizenship. So there are four actions that we understand as fundamental: political will, coordination, consensus and awareness of citizenship. A regulation that leads us to a proper management of waste prevents them from ending in the marine environment, and policies that prevent the deposit of biodegradable waste in landfills will help reduce greenhouse gas emissions. So for us, a new waste law in Galicia must have four main objectives: to set the principles of the circular economy in terms of waste, to contribute to the fight against climate change, to protect our marine environment and to contribute to the creation and consolidation of jobs in the waste management sector. And after these lines are a little more generic and I'm focusing a little bit on the articulate of this bill, we must not forget that European environmental legislation is very dynamic and is constantly moving forward. So the delay that led to the processing of this bill — which started almost three years ago — has caused, among other things, that this bill does not appear in line with current European policies on waste management. And I refer to European Directive 2019/904 of 5 June 2019 on the reduction of the impact of certain plastic products on the environment. On the contrary, if there appears the preliminary draft law that is being dealt with in the Congress of Deputies. At this point, we cannot understand a waste management regulation that leaves out the new requirements regarding the management of plastic use. Plastic, as we know, is a material that is very present in our lives, which has beneficial effects — such as food safety or transport ease — but which also presents many and serious environmental problems, as it is a material that is fragmented into many particles and which deteriorates very slowly. The European Union has set out a clear strategy to move towards a more circular plastic sector and, above all, to fight against marine pollution from these plastics. This is one of the main reasons for our amendment, because we do not understand how this European regulation is not included in this management; and above all, in terms of Galicia, a place where we have great marine wealth and where it affects much more of this problem. Marine animals are ingesting a lot of microplastics. And in addition, by transferring into the food chain, this causes them to end up in our dishes, also affecting human health. On the other hand, there is also the waste accumulated on the beaches, which directly affects the tourist sector and which also costs the Administration in cleaning work — among many of the things we already know about plastics—. On the other hand, according to data from the 2016-2022 Framework State Waste Management Plan, it is established that selective collection and differentiated treatment of organic matter of domestic origin implies 42% of municipal competition waste, so we understand that deadlines should be cut and set as an objective, both for smaller and larger municipalities, the separation of bioresidues before December 31, 2021, thus adapting to our territorial reality. Because you have to combine different formulas, those that can be the most suitable at each place — some places can be composting or others can be the collected door-to-door, and apply above all and essentially the guiding principle that those who pollute pay. With regard to local authorities, this law should require greater inter-administrative coordination, regulate mechanisms to achieve sufficient funding and enable municipalities to participate in autonomic plans, taking into account the municipalities and their specialties for their drafting. On the other hand, in relation to the waste management model, we reject the concentration model, the Sogama model, which is still present in this bill. We choose – – always without prejudice to the application of the management hierarchy, which must prevail – by a model of adequacy and proximity. We bet on the creation of a network for waste recovery for the remaining fraction, as well as for energy recovery and the use or treatment of rejection; a network that allows waste recovery and, in case it is not possible, its disposal in more appropriate and nearby facilities, which provide service to a specific territorial area and which use appropriate technologies and means in each case to ensure a high level of protection for public health and the environment. We consider it more appropriate to assemble a given territorial scope to achieve a better economies of scale, without excluding, for specific areas, the bet on smaller and local or comarcal facilities distributed across the territory. On the other hand, they would also help generate jobs in these different territories. And we reject the incineration by the environmental impact that it produces as a solution. We understand that prevention is fundamental. Within an efficient waste policy, prevention measures are essential by including concrete and quantifiable targets for the prevention of waste generation, which I believe could be more ambitious in this law and have a greater tax burden. It has to be done very well in reducing packaging by promoting the use of drinking water sources and reusable packaging, especially in sectors such as accommodation or catering. Specific articles should be included in which prevention measures are collected, which appear in the directive mentioned above, on the promotion of sustainable production and consumption models, on the design of products which are efficient and durable in terms of useful life, as well as on the reduction of the generation of waste in the industrial sector, on mineral extraction or on construction, by taking into account the best available techniques. As far as environmental taxation is concerned, it is necessary by establishing tax figures that have to constitute a complementary economic instrument to combine environmental protection and defence, which, although they are certainly not politically profitable at first, if they are necessary to stimulate and encourage more respectful behaviors with the natural environment and can be combined with other figures, such as subsidies or tax exemptions for private companies...
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u24	Quiet, please.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u25	...that they meet certain environmental objectives. It is also necessary to increase transparency in waste management. A basic principle in this sense must be transparency towards citizenship. The citizenry has to know and receive clearly and disaggregated what it pays for and why it does it. And it has to be rewarded with its own effort towards a positive, supportive and environmentally friendly attitude. For this purpose, all concepts and services, which in each case include public prices, rates or rates of collection and treatment of waste, should be shaped. With regard to the involvement of the councils and the citizenship, the ruling of the Economic and Social Council accompanying this draft law criticised in its general considerations, on the one hand, the lack of real measures for environmental adequacy and training, the lack of coordination with other autonomous communities, the insufficient funding of the councils or the lack of ambition in the administration's obligations to ensure access to information. In addition, I also criticized the fact that it was not easy for municipalities to participate in the planning process. This law therefore needs to also focus on aspects such as the need to share citizenship in this work through the creation of awareness-raising campaigns for integration into programmes which are established, for example, for the protection of the marine environment or for water. It is also necessary to improve information available to citizens on prevention in waste generation, their proper separation in origin, possible reuse and recycling, campaigns that promote active participation in waste production reduction actions, about their danger, preparedness actions for reuse or in the implementation of selective collection. The Deputy of Pontevedra has campaigned to inform, raise awareness and raise awareness of the municipalities about composting which, in the end, is causing the implementation of this system in the different small municipalities to have a high degree of success among the population. That is why, given these precedents to which I refer — from the Pontevedra Parliament — it does not implicate the Union in the mistake of attempting to impose without first informing and raising awareness. And in addition, taking into account what the Government of the United States likes these great advertising campaigns of automobiles, they could also use them for these information and awareness-raising work for efficient waste management. And finally, I wanted to include in my intervention -- because I don't think we can extract them from this regulation -- the new circumstances in which we find ourselves. And I mean the pandemic of the vault-19. The struggle against this pandemic has also led to the increase of certain types of waste; in particular, plastics of one use such as gloves, covers or protective screens, which are no longer used only in the health system, but we are also using them right now in the street. The demand for these products has multiplied, and this also poses a serious pollution problem because many of them are non-recyclable — and I mean gossip — or because in other cases, being recyclable, on many occasions it is not done. Even though I hope this new normality we find is temporary and it doesn't happen again, it doesn't come to stay, I think it would be good to take it into account for a regulation, since this law is still in processing. So we're at the moment of being able to include it, because if we don't efficiently regulate the management of these new resources, the consequences for the environment can be devastating. No more, thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u49	Thank you very much, Mr Bará. We now move on to the next point, which is 1.3. Debate taken into account by the G.P. Galician people, amending Law 8/2008 of 10 July 2008 on health of Galician citizens
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u50	For the presentation of the non-law motion, by the People's Parliamentary Group of Galicia, Mr. Puy Fraga has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u51	Mr. President, ladies and gentlemen. Very good morning to all and all. It is an honour for me to present today, before this House, this bill amending the health law of Galicia, Law 8/2008. It's probably one of the projects, within the many bills and legislative proposals this House is debating at the beginning of this legislation, of which we consider most important. This bill has clear objectives, which are to cover the ponds and deficiencies of the vigorous legislation to fight better against the pandemic; a health situation that turns out to be, for us, for our generation, completely new, unpredictable and, for its duration, a long epidemic, for its persistence in time, and even because it is a situation that we all think can be recurrent, not only in the short term but with regard to the coming years. When talking about the pandemic, it is necessary once again to remember the latest data we have in Galicia: there are over 52,000 people suffering or suffering from infection; this second wave, fortunately, is being less intense, both in Galicia and Spain, than was the first, the difference from what is happening in other European countries; Galicia, within Spain, also continues to have a smaller cumulative incidence, both seven days and fourteen days - the last one, the fourteen days, which we are with the latest data made public by the Ministry - is 178 versus 215 of the average of Spain; and we also have less positivity. This less bad data, of course, doesn't mean that we don't have to keep working and, of course, always carry in memory the more than 1,200 dead in Galicia, always bear in mind their suffering, those of their families, those of their friends, with whom we share their pain, and, of course, always carry in memory all those who are fighting against the pandemic in whatever places it is. We all want to give them courage and strength, and also all those who are still infected today. As a society we have a ring, and there's a light at the end of the tunnel, which are the vaccines, which are already beginning to think about their distribution, but there are still months left. The Spanish Government itself announced that this until summer would not be resolved. We must therefore continue to behave with responsibility throughout this season and always bear in mind those who suffer from illness, but also their economic and social consequences, and we must do everything possible to minimize the consequences of this unprecedented situation in many years. Precisely, the bill we understand is an exercise of responsibility because it accumulates the management experience since March by the Autonomic Administration; a period in which it was pointed out that our health policy framework was not prepared to face a long pandemic, with widespread effects on society, itself and ethymologically a pandemic, both on public health and on the health system as a whole of society; that is, both in economic, social and even political aspects, because we saw and experienced in the first person the effects on the very functioning of the democratic system. As we know, the regulatory framework is characterized by a constitution that provides for states of emergency, specifically the state of alarm for pandemic situations. It was an optimal application of the Constitution at first, and it allowed to face the first collision, the first wave of the pandemic. My group has always agreed to that alarm status application. Our party supported the first two months four consecutive votes; the votes of the People's Party allowed that statement of alarm status, but the fact is that that alarm state is underdeveloped. The organic law governing the states of alarm, exception and site, which is organic law 4/81, is referred to in Article 12 for cases of health crises - in Article 4(b) such as epidemics, to which the competent authority, when the state of alarm is declared competent, may adopt by itself, according to the cases, the measures laid down in the rules for the fight against infectious diseases, and the fact is that the health laws, both national and national - organic law 3/1986, on health - such as the Galician Health Act, which is Law 8/2008, do not provide for pandemic situations such as those we are currently living. So there was an attempt to reform the State Organic Law. It was proposed at the Conference of Presidents early in May that there was even a commitment to reform before the second state of alarm by the Government of Spain and finally did not take place, despite the fact that the Ditome of the Congressional Reactivation Commission – – which was drawn up in June– July– advised to implement this reform. But only the legal problems raised during the first months of the pandemic were solved, and the disparity of the judicial review of the measures taken by the various self-governments at the co-governance stage prior to the second state of alarm showed the need to make reforms, as one that was done - which was correct - which was the reform of the Law on Dispute-Administrative Jurisdiction, so that the competence of the review of anti-ronovirus measures adopted by the autonomous communities would be carried out by the higher courts of justice. There was no legislative action in the rest. I think it's obvious that two problems arose at the co-governance stage. First of all, the strictly legal ones. There's an auto from the Madrid High Court of Justice that constitutes a clear call for attention to the need to resort to law rather than to ministerial orders or advice orders to restrict the exercise of fundamental rights. And I remember what that car said. It literally says: "The fundamental rights conferred by the Constitution on citizens cannot be affected by any state interference not authorized by their representatives by a rule of law that meets the minimum requirements required by the legal certainty requirements." Moreover, the judgment advises the use of instruments that provide for the rule of law - which are the laws - to legislate in pandemic situations when health authorities are forced to restrict the exercise of fundamental rights. The second call of attention was already in the second state of alarm, also in the period of co-governance, when some autonomous community, for example, Asturias -- in which there was exemplary behavior in the first wave, but which was especially affected by the second -- its autonomous government, called for total house confinement in its territory. The Ministry of Health then requested that it be expected, said that the total house confinements were not planned and that they would therefore have to wait for the effects of the intermediate measures being taken by the various autonomous communities. The fact is that the period of co-governance has been contradicted by the fact that an autonomous community has asked for a measure which has not been adopted, because neither was it provided for in the organic law nor in the corresponding autonomous law. It is finally necessary to give legal certainty, both to the administrations and to the administrations. It was not possible to update the state law, although we ask it here from this House, but we can reform the autonomous law; of course, by also fulfilling the fifth recommendation of the Congressional Social and Economic Reconstruction Commission, which approved, with the vote of the parties represented here in its health part, the fifth recommendation on "...upgrading national and autonomous health legislation", giving priority to those who would be directed against a possible second outbreak of the pandemic, which is what we are living in. Finally, we are proposing a legislative debate that wants to be projected for what remains of the pandemic, but also to stop the possible pandemics that may come in the future. In the face of improvisation, uncertainty and disparity of legal criteria, we believe that regulation by law guarantees the automatism of responses, reduces uncertainties in administrations about what the Administration can do and in what circumstances and allows a more homogeneous interpretation by the courts of proportionality and the adequacy of the provisions that the health authority adopts, as well as resolves – – – we are trying to solve – the problems of competence, as has been raised in Galicia regarding sanctions. And precisely this bill, the first thing it does is to define which authorities exercise jurisdiction in the event of a pandemic. It is therefore established, and it delineates who is the health authority, which includes from the Board of Trustees, the person who holds the Health Council – – who today accompanies us, and that is why we also thank him – to the persons who hold the Service Inspection, to the holders of the territorial health offices and to the mayors and mayors. Secondly, with this bill we also list the measures which may be taken by the health authorities, ranging from the seizure and immobilization of products - something which is provided for in the state of alarm, but not in the Greek Health Act - to measures of all kinds: delimitations of capacity, security measures and hygiene in certain places or activities, self-protection measures or intervention in social services centres or the provision of data necessary for the control and containment of the risk to public health. Thirdly, this bill also provides preventive measures of recognition, treatment, hospitalization or control when there is supposed to be a danger to the health of the population or to a person or group of people. We provide for home isolation, hospitalization at a hospital or other detention, the submission to quarantiners at home or in another suitable place of persons with illness, or measures to restrict or restrict the movement or mobility of persons within an area affected in certain places of the home or in certain time slots. We also anticipate the intervention of social services centres, as soon as it has been found that this is an essential measure for the protection of public health, especially for older people and people with disabilities. In addition, these measures will be taken with the urgency required, without, of course, avoiding their subsequent control by the judiciary. Thirdly, it lays down guarantees for the protection of the rights of citizens. Obviously, respect for the dignity of the person is imposed as an absolute limit, and it provides for a number of rules of action, such as those concerning which should be the least intrusive and invasive possible in order to achieve the public health objective, but it is also envisaged that they are temporary, that they are transparent and that they are motivated in accordance with three rules: the suitability, necessity and proportionality of the adopted standard. Fourthly, and finally, three questions are introduced. The proposed law for the amendment of the Galician Health Act pays special attention to new technologies, such as the mechanism for information, prevention, detection and follow-up of diseases, something that this new pandemic has just proved to be a useful tool that can be used to improve the situation, follow-up and tracking of the pandemic. Secondly, it also requires, in some ways, or refers to the necessary collaboration between the Government of Greece and the municipalities, which must provide information and provide the necessary cooperation with one another. And finally, the sanctioning regime will be clarified with a clearer and more complete sanctioning regime than preventing the rules from being met, because, among other things, even if sanctions are imposed, they are not effective. Ladies and gentlemen, social psychologists say there are three ways of dealing with a pandemic, like any other kind of misfortune that affects an individual or a group of people. There's a first answer, which is negationism. In politics we have seen denial by the American Administration, and all we can say about this is that it is politically regrettable. There's a second answer, which is self-referential, which tries one to worry about himself, how it can affect the situation and how it can get out of it. This attitude, from a political point of view, we think it is clearly irresponsible. And there's a third way to deal with a tragedy like the pandemic we suffer, which is being effective and focusing on knowing and analysing the situation well to find the most effective ways to solve the problems. That is the position that we believe must prevail in this House, and that is why we put forward this debate, which is genuinely legislative, to determine among all of us how to act in front of a pandemic, with the law in hand, with all possible guarantees that will minimize its impact on public health and on social, economic, personal and all kinds of relationships, both in the present and in the future. We hope, ladies and gentlemen, as always, that this genuinely legislating debate will be a constructive debate, with useful approaches, which, of course, we will try to incorporate into the rule finally adopted by the House. Thank you very much.
