ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u120	This motion we now present is a continuation of the interpellation that we addressed to the Councillor for Culture, Education and University in the past full, concerning the demand for a plan to reactivate the Greek cultural sector. In it we present, as you know, an integral set of eleven measures that really involve us with the necessary momentum of the Greek culture as an engine of economic revival; eleven measures that I will not reiterate here because they are at your disposal in the corresponding document, but which I would like, yes, to simplify into five major ideas: the first is the continued search for dialogue with the cultural sector; the second is the search for a plan of action that serves the entire cultural chain; the third, the reactivation of production and creation units; the fourth, the declaration of culture as essential; and, finally, the adaptation of the rules of promotion to health progress, as well as the reformulation of Xacobeo. Every week, the uncertainty grows. And a year that has already been lost for nine months for the cultural sectors is opening up to another new year that is about to come in that the questioners weigh on each project that either could not have been born or could not have developed in its entirety: cancellation schedules, persistent deferral maps, a zero-kilometre chronology for workers and cultural workers who hardly live with regular figures of rent payments, credits, wages or social contributions that say well with the necessary relief that cultural management needs to take care of each creative process. Because culture is born from the effort to create it, and talent works. Don't forget, there's no divine inspiration. Today's culture is the memory of our people, who will remain far beyond us. And it will also speak for us of our acts in matters of collective consciousness, of thinking and feeling, of educating and forming society in that feeling and critical thinking. They are times coveted for Greek culture like sound for many other sectors that require priority, preferential, essential and political attention so that, despite everything, be this time of careful containment of our cultural values and training to continue with their development adapted to new circumstances; for once, that we should see how seedy we must feed from a responsible government that truly believes in culture as a right and as an essential good for society. There are too many reasons why we should strive from the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group in the imperative need to offer to the cultural sectors as well as to society as a whole — because culture is a fundamental cultural substrate for the formation of a people freed from the barbarism of inculturation — that we offer them a plan of genuine reactivation and cultural impulse that acts at present, but that also promotes the latex of the future. "The continuous present is the only verbal time of commitment." In the Galego Nationalist Block, we believe in these verses of writer Maria Reimondez, and we work against a conditional future, conditioned in cultural matters in the country's key. We have already discussed here the data provided by the report of the Galeg Culture Council in the statistical setting of the cultural field last October. But today from the Galego Nationalist Block, what we bring to this plenary is a deeper reflection, which is the maintenance of the aid service in the extraordinary home, which, as you know, ended last November 30th, to address together the design and management of the home aid service in our country. That's why we also tabled an amendment to replace the Socialist Party's initiative with the text of our own. The extraordinary SAF — I say and I insist — ended on 30 November; in fact, this Monday, on 7 December, the Galician Board communicated to the municipalities an extension in more than one million hours of the SAF linked to the Dependency Law for 2021, which will be distributed from 15 this month, under the same conditions as it has been developing until now, i.e., with an economic contribution from the Greek Government of 9.7 euros/hour, indeed, lent to those who are directly linked to the Dependency Law and without more criteria for distribution of those hours between the various municipalities that — literally the communication of the Union — "the needs detected by the Greek Government itself in accordance with the information collected in the SIGAD". And that's the first of the issues we wanted to bring today from the BNG to this plenary, because the Galician Union has the wrong habit — and it does not do it solely with services and social policy, it does so in many other areas as well — of making decisions that affect the municipalities but without counting at all either their opinions or their considerations, or much less on the situation that local authorities have to deal with. He approved, as indicated a moment ago, half a million hours of extraordinary SAF unilaterally; he also unilaterally communicated that he ended the service on November 30, without asking, without contrasting what needs and what reality they had the various 300 municipalities of our country; and now he does the same again, he distributes more hours. We do not deny the need for that extension or that increase of hours, but again it does so unilaterally and without the opinion of the municipalities themselves, with no more information or more contrast than an information poster hanging from the Social Services website. We believe that the debate that now takes place — and it is already a debate that comes from old or far away — is precisely to speak and address the conditions in which the home help service is being provided today, how it is financed, how it is managed and, above all, that it must change to ensure the highest quality in the care of users, but above all, how to ensure the best working conditions for the people who are developing that work. We talk a lot about this chamber where legislation is concerned — and before now — because it was essential, because it was necessary, of the situation of the older residences in our country, of the conditions in which the workers and workers of the residences develop their work, but we have to take into account that one of the main ones — not to say the principal — the attention service to dependent people in Galicia, is precisely this, is the home aid service, especially in those interior and rural areas of the country that do not have other proximity services; nearly twenty-four thousand users in 2019 who are cared for in their homes. I also want to make an incision here, because the degree of coverage, being one of the main services, which has the SAF in Galicia, is below the average of the Spanish state. We have 2.98% per 100 people over 65, compared to 3.99% in the case of the average of the Spanish State. We have agreed since BNG: the SAF is a fundamental service, it is a very necessary service, but it holds itself upon a huge precariousness. What do we propose today from the BNG with this initiative and, above all, with which two objectives we want or call to review this service? The first is to demand that dialogue of what he was talking about at the beginning, to demand that dialogue of the Board with the Town Councils, through the entity that represents them all, which is the Fegamp: that any substantial change, whether an increase of hours or of any other kind, must be the result of a dialogue and also of a will at least of consensus and negotiation, not of unilateral attitudes or decisions. You will remember that in the previous plenary session since the BNG, in a question that we asked the Social Policy Adviser, we asked him to keep this time increase that assumed the extraordinary SAF once that program was over. But we also insisted very much on the advisor that it was necessary for the maintenance of hours to be the result of dialogue and coordination with the municipalities, because it is the local entities that ultimately have to manage this service. Secondly, the second objective of this initiative is to further unify the conditions under which it lends itself and improve the quality of the service, mainly through the guarantee and recognition of more and better labour rights for workers in this home aid service, almost 100% of them, especially in the category of assistants, women. And also because of this feminization of service, like most of the services that have to do with care in our country, to a large extent, it is also invisible, precarious and extremely devalued for the great social benefit it brings to the whole society of this country. What do we propose from the BNG with this initiative that changes the service? In two aspects basically: in terms of funding, but also in an important part of the organisation of the SAF itself. In the financing, essentially, going towards an increase in the contribution that the Galician Union makes to adjust it to the actual cost of providing the service and also ensuring with that increase a decent wage for workers. Today we find ourselves in many municipalities in this working country of the SAF that are receiving a salary below what marks a collective agreement that, as you know, is expired in 2011. And besides, that contribution made by the Galician Board, that 9.70 euros/hour, actually borrowed, is very far away and is totally insufficient to cope with the actual cost of the service itself. Mrs Ortega said she had a price, a cost of 13 euros, and really between 18 and 23 euros per hour, according to that report prepared by the Official College of Social Work in Galicia. That low funding, that is to say, that the Union only provides €9.7 per hour, which is favouring job insecurity and that many SAF workers receive a wage below — I insist — from what marks the agreement itself, in addition to demanding a huge effort to local authorities, because they have to assume the difference between the real cost of service provision and the part that makes Joina. Based on the fact that users and users are getting closer depending on income and wealth they have, of course, in the country as is Galicia, with the second lowest pensions in the state, logically — we also want to make it clear — most of the users or do not contribute anything or make the minimum contribution, according to — I insist — their income and their heritage. That's what it's got to do with funding. And the part of the service organization that we propose today since the BNG is, first, that objective criteria are established for the distribution of these hour increments, that is not a unilateral and discretionary decision of the Galician Union, but that it responds to a contrast and to a prior negotiation with the municipalities so that progress is adjusted also in the incorporation of those hours and even the increase itself to the needs of the municipalities and their capacity also as service providers to deal with that time increase. Also modify the regulations to require that most of those home help services, which in our country are managed by private companies, have to have more technical and coordination staff and therefore have to reduce the ratio of technical staff according to the number of users and users to adjust it to a higher quality in the provision of the service. I also improve the working conditions of the assistants, with an organization of the working day that doesn't go according to the hours, but has another unit of measurement. And why would we say that? Because today full-time contracts for home help workers are the exception rather than the norm. The most common thing is that we find ourselves in a five-hour, six-hour period, which makes it very difficult to reconcile personal life, work life and professional life of these people, and that they are taking many women working in the home aid service to earn wages under the SMI. And what we can't do is, neither before the pandemic nor much less after what we've lived in this country, to support rights and social services on labour exploitation and denial of rights to workers and workers. Improving working conditions, which also involve intervention and prevention protocols on situations that are increasingly common in the homes of users, where cases of discrimination, cases of sexual assault or even racism occur. And what we are asking is that protocols be drawn up and that the regulatory reforms be made that are necessary in order to be able to temporarily or absolutely suspend the service in those cases where, indeed, this violation of rights takes place. Today the regulation only provides for temporary suspension of the service in two cases which have nothing to do with the working conditions. And ended with a final reflection, with the life expectancy we have. Immersed as we are in this chamber in the debate about demographic impulse, we have to strengthen services and care and, above all, everything that has to do with the attention of dependents, especially those people who have will, who so desire and have the right to stay for as long as possible in their own homes; it's their right. But above all, we also have to bear in mind that social well-being and that caretakers are workers, but that job has to be of good quality and decent working conditions. We've been taking this initiative since BNG today. We think it's a little step, even if it's small, on that way to guarantee better services, of greater proximity, of higher quality and, above all, with more rights. Nothing else.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u188	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, very late. Ferrol was born bound to the sea. His birth as a city is due to a decision by the state; a decision taken by his natural and strategic characteristics: a narrow, deep, easy to defend and close to the main Atlantic routes. This is how you create a shipyard to build ships and an arsenal to house them, and then you develop a city, the city of Ferrol, the departmental city. The first great challenge in the manufacture of ships was the simultaneous construction of the twelve Apostles in 1751, twelve ships in scale launched only in two years. So for almost three centuries, the city, the Navy and Navantia formed a single reality and united their destiny, forging the DNA of thousands of ferrolans from different generations. This proposal that we present to the plenary today does not deal with the past; too much has already been said, too argumentary of party. The success is there, and I don't think anyone's proud of it. It's not even about the present, although it's hard for us to subtract from it when in the last year 1,047 people have lost their jobs in the naval, and the prospects are even worse by March next year, because for the first time in a long time we will present totally empty grades. And that's what we've been experiencing difficult times: to use 15,000 people on the yards a little more than 2,000 right now! We need plans and immediate workload, so we all agree. But the state budgets have already found that the only new construction for the Navy will be in the south, and that the AOR, which we all demanded unanimously in Ferrol, does not appear in the state budget. The situation is so dramatic that, from our point of view, it doesn't admit that the trees let us see the forest. The naval needs to be built around a single strategic, solvent and possible project at this point in time to ensure the future of a city and a region. That's why we need everyone's sum, instead of discussing what separates us. It's what the Greeks and the Greeks that we represent and also the ferrolans expect. It's always said — and it's true — that people are the greatest value in protecting a community or a city. Today Ferrol has 66,000 inhabitants, the same as he had in 1950. We are the only city in Spain that has lost a third of its population in the last forty years. This is a real demographic crisis. We count on the highest rate of unemployment in Galicia, 17.9%, and the crisis in the naval sector now sums up the energy crisis, the decisions on Endesa, Alcoa and a long et cetera. From the industrial energy crisis we went to the crisis in the city itself. A recent study believes that in the center of it, in the Magdalena neighborhood, we have 313 empty commercial sites. Finally, after an economic crisis, a demographic crisis, an industrial crisis, comes the hardest, which is the social crisis. Unfortunately, Ferrol is also the city of Galicia with the highest rate of poverty, 24%. That is why he said that continuing to discuss a situation of industrial and social emergency instead of joining us in order to change the situation is to waste time, and above all to fail to the confidence of the people who deposit it in this Parliament. Just like saying that things are going well, that the frigates are already coming, it's an irresponsibility if we don't make reality the strategic project around what our future turns, not the future of the next ten years, but the future to be competitive in the coming decades; a project so that we can continue to do what we've always done, diversifying, yes, but also doing what we've always done, what we've always done is build ships; a project that our children don't have to leave the city that saw them born. We can discuss diversification, wind, new industrial plans and employment, skills... we'll probably have different views around these issues. We can discuss publicly, privately, civilianly, militaryly, Montoro, Montero... what we want, but without a project of dam and without a project of transformation of the Starry 4.0, Ferrol will have no future. That project and its financing — in Ferrol no one doubt — corresponds to the owner of the shipyards, which is the State. This is how the Starship 4.0 project was born: it was born as it was born in the most important shipyards in the world. In Ferrol was born from the hand of magnificent industrial engineers trained in our university schools and Navantia, who emulated what Jorge Juan did three centuries ago by the Marquis of the Ensenada: to visit the most modern shipyards in the world to copy many of his constructive techniques. This was also done in the 19th century with the great transformation of steel and steam ships, with the incorporation of machinery or dry construction, in which Navantia — its precedent — was a genuine advance of a world character. And so it was also done, for the last time, in the early 19th century after the Ferrandiz Law, with a project -- you have to say -- identical to what is being said right now, a reform of the yard and new curated to the Navy. A century later we need a new technological leap, not for ten years, but for the next 100 years. In our history, we're seeing how every 100 years we have to do a great project to transform the yards, and in Ferrol we've grown up with this over the last few years. As a century ago, it includes the modernization of the most important fleet units by the hand of the F-110 frigates. One data: with the start of the Starry 4.0 we will save 36 million euros for each frigate, allowing the necessary investment of 400 million euros for this project to get a return from its investment to the ten years of the start. We don't want to forget that even if a yard is public, it has to be competitive to stay on the market, and in this Starry 4.0 transformation project three key investments appear: the dry dike covered 360 by 65 meters — about what rotates the entire project — the sublocus workshop and the expansion of three new springes of 273, 200 and 350 meters. This project received technical financial support from the Galician Union through two joint research units in which, in addition to the Union and Navantia, participated at the University of Coruña. They worked on lines of work, optimizing design and construction processes, increasing the degree of optimization and robotization, and improving coordination with the auxiliary industry. The project was completed in 2018, after eight technical reviews under the Navantia presidency of ferrolán Esteban Vilasánchez, and many people participated in dozens of technical meetings with the General Environmental Assessment Directorate and the territorial Institute of Studies. I'm going to be able to point out the importance of this project for the city, because it also has a direct return to an old yearning for all the railways, which is to open the city at sea, since the project of transformation of the yard also allows to release a part of our maritime facade. The truth is, everything was frozen with the change in the Spanish Government. Esteban Vilasanchez has died and has been named an Andalusian agronomist. And as on so many occasions in our history, Navantia's Strategic Plan once again looks south. The project promoter, Navantia, Sepi and the state, with the new Government, what they were doing was to be dizzy about who the noun organ was; that is, with whom the project was working with. They ended up passing it to the Ferrol Port, but the reality is that two years later they didn't even deposit the avalanche to begin the processing. This is the reality. Two years later, the environmental impact study is about to finish, and in the meantime, the Ferrol naval smolders and the city is in a real state of social, economic and labor emergency. That is why this proposal, so that with a firm voice the Galician Parliament urges the Galician Council to call on the Government of the State to submit the projects, licences and authorizations, as well as the financing of the 4.0 Starring, especially as regards the covered dry dam. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u190	Thank you, President. Ladies and gentlemen, the situation in the naval sector is complicated, and much more so in these times of cowardice. The fall in demand for maritime transport and goods and transit is having negative effects on civil naval procurement and repair this year, while in the naval defence market the budgetary and fiscal difficulties and effects, although they have not been fully reflected in this exercise, significant falls are already seen in all geographic areas of the naval, military and civilian recruitment sum; 42% in China, 71% in Japan, 55% in Korea and a 97% drop in recruitment in Europe. The impact is also on the collaborative industry and on the equipment suppliers, who had to reduce or stop their production, and in addition to implementing rigorous safety and health measures during coronavirus co-operation. Another significant impact was on shipowners, in particular on cruise operators, due to the intense fall of tourism passengers, which caused delays in orders and the total fall in demand on shipyards in Europe. The forecasts indicate that the overall impact of the crisis on the maritime industry will be more intense from 2021 because of the lack of orders and the lack of workload. In the defence sector, although it is unknown to what extent the international market will be affected, the following effects are seen: a replanning of military naval programmes provided for by budgetary adjustments, a fall in the export market in Middle East countries motivated by the collapse of oil prices and a global tendency to protect and develop each country's respective industrial bases, with the aim of ensuring sovereignty in the supply chain. [[One person from the House's cleaning services comes to disinfect the floor.]] G.P.'s non-legislative proposal. of the Galego Nationalist Block, at the initiative of D. Xosé Luís Bará Torres and two other Members, on the demands to be made in relation to the development in Galicia of the Atlantic freight corridor
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u221	There is an amendment by the People's Parliamentary Group.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u222	To formulate the non-law proposal, you have the floor to Mrs. Fernández Gómez.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u223	Good afternoon. Thank you, President. We are presenting this initiative with the objective that this chamber be positioned unanimously — hopefully — on two central issues for our country. First of all, what strategy should Galicia have with regard to the European commodity infrastructure network, and also be able to define the design and layout of the network that best enables us to organize economic space in the service of the country's interests. We believe it's also important to be able to decide on those projects that must be priority in order to get that strategy forward. We're at the door of a new financial framework 2021-2027 and the central government seems to be unaware. This parliament believes that it must be positioned to demand the state's immediate development of the Atlantic corridor, understanding as projects the whole development of institutional frameworks where the Greek agents and the Government themselves can participate in decision-making, including investment planning and, of course, the investment needed to move it forward. Because the statements and agreements are worth a short while if there's no money or will. The European corridors are part of a redefinition of the geopolitical framework of enormous silence, which affects us and affects us as a country. That is why I think it is necessary to open this debate by complementing the immediate and urgent agenda with a contextualization of the underlying process in which we are immersed. As far as Galicia is concerned, there are three key dynamics that are determining and defining the geopolitical role of our country. The first of these is the implementation of this neoliberal project and its consequences in space terms. Since the 1980s there have been new patterns of inequality. On the one hand, there is a growing concentration and accumulation around large agglomerations and, on the other hand, the average cities lose more and more weight. That means a new spatial referral that especially affects our country, a polycentric, polynucleate country, with medium cities. Geographical inequalities are growing in the state as a whole, and ultimately a new division of work is producing. On the other hand, this neoliberal project and its consequences in space terms also involve re-articulation and transformation of scales. We have passed from the industrialization policies that sought a certain cohesion and internal state homogenization towards a model of large metropolises operating within an architectural economy model, where these large metropolises compete with each other on a global scale but are increasingly untiring from its contour. And the lesson we can learn from this process that's under way is that they're holding out better those areas that have either a large metropolis or they have regions that are made up of a set of large medium cities with a high level of cohesion. We have that mid-Polycentric city system, which we don't have is that necessary internal vertebration. We are also part of one of the major European regions, the Atlantic axis, but what we lack is internal vertebration. We also have the enormous geopolitical potential to be at the center of Atlantic communications, but we can't develop it either. Because this whole potential collides with the other two key dynamics that explain our role today, which are the geopolitical strategy of the European Union and the geopolitical strategy of the state, which conditions the concrete application of that neoliberal model in our territory. The second of these keys is therefore, as I said, the European Space Strategy. It was well-figured in this 1989 document with the title of European Megalopolis, drawn up by the French Agency DATAR, which distinguishes this space known as the "blue banana" — the central area that goes from London to Milan, the so-called hard core of the European Union —. [[Mrs. Fernández Gómez shows a document.]] And in that strategy it was determined that this great European metropolis, at risk of congestion, should launch the expansion and creation of peripheral colonies to increase wealth. Well, this is the basis of the European territorial model. I know there will be anyone who will tell me: no, but if another polycentric model was introduced in the official documents of the European Territorial Management Perspective. The reality is that this polycentric model was only actually applied to what is that blue banana space. That conception, that model I had before, is in fact the basis of what was then applied to the European corridors. [[Mrs. Fernández Gómez shows a document.]] This strategy adopted in 2013, with this polycentric model and a dense mesh of connections in the hard core, that European hard core, which allows that thick mesh, also allows to encourage regional dynamics and also to encourage dynamics of metropolitan specialization. However, the periphery, peripheral radial corridors have a very different articulated layout, articulated around corridors where the commodity flows of a limited number of ports are concentrated. This type of trace, in front of the mesh, has the weakness that limits the ability to create territorial cohesion dynamics on a regional scale. Well, considering that our ability to be more competitive depends on the greater vertebration of the medium cities in regional terms and on the articulation of the continuous Atlantic axis, the second lesson we can learn is that this type of model — the corridor model — is insufficient for our needs. We didn't need the model that was introduced in 2013, when it didn't even include us in the corridors, but we also find it insufficient to change the layout approved in 2019, with that branch that goes from the Greek ports to León. It's a step; it's an important step to connect to the network. But our medium-term objective must be to expand the network and bet on a continuous and cohesioned Atlantic axis, enabling the development of the potential of a region that adds up to the network six ports, from Ferrol to Sines, three of them of great silence. What we're considering is fundamental: moving from a purely radial model to the Meseta to a model combined with the coastal corridor. And this model of coastal corridor is nothing revolutionary. In fact, the coastal corridor model is the model of all peripheral spaces in Europe. They have it in Italy, they have it in Greece, they have it in Ireland... all those who were later known as PIC have coastal corridors, including the Portuguese facade, but the Spanish State doesn't. And here we come to the spatial strategy of the Spanish State. Because if all the states tried to encourage those enclaves who had competitive advantages and better conditions to capture resources, they tried to enhance, therefore, their ports and their internal connection, the anomaly was the proposal of the Spanish State for the Atlantic corridor. And here, as I say, is the third key dynamic, which is the geopolitical strategy of the state, increasingly radial. In this 2013 proposal there was a minimum version of this radial model, which was to connect only the ports of the entire state to Madrid, the port of Bilbao and the port of Alxeciras, the only Atlantic ports connected by the state. And they refused to connect a single port from Porto to Bilbao. And because of that decision we lost the opportunity to count on the European Union's 2014 to 2020 investment programme. This decision is a consequence of this geopolitical function that the State grants to the Atlantic corridor, which is reduced exclusively to that which Madrid operates as a node in the peninsula context. We are concerned that in practice this vision should continue to operate, because, despite the adoption by the European Parliament in 2019 of the La Coruña-Vigo-Ourense-Leon — in which it was agreed that it should be carried out through the existing sections that already belonged to the core network — the reality is that, after this, nothing has been done on the part of the Central Government. Therefore, the inclusion of this frame in the basic network must take into account that it requires, according to the European Regulation, to meet a whole range of requirements that are essential to form part of that network: issues that have to do with the electrification of the line, with the minimum load of the axis, with the speed, the length of the train, with the security system and the wide track... ultimately, the task is ingenious and we are concerned because, at the door of the new fund period of 2020-2027, nothing moves in the central government. With this initiative we want to demand precisely this, that the central government let the politics of arms fall. We want guarantees that in January 2021 projects will be presented for the development of this branch of the Atlantic corridor, because we hear the Central Government announce their commitment day and day also with the Mediterranean corridor, but on the contrary, absolute silence regarding the Atlantic corridor. We are concerned that in practice we are again free from funding, because there are clear symptoms that central government priorities are not the same for all sections of the state. If not, how do you explain that Adif has a technical office for the management of the Mediterranean corridor and that it does not even exist for the Atlantic? So we demand strategy and projects. The strategy, as I said, should be moving forward in the medium term to include new steps to enable us to articulate the Atlantic axis. And according to that strategy, I mean that the southern exit — as we have discussed recently — is a fundamental piece for which we can, of course, already count on European co-financing, as a cross-border framework. What are you waiting for, gentlemen of the Socialist Party? We need to start the previous information study and not go backwards with alternative studies. The immediate strategy is to advance in the elaboration by the Central Government of a master plan of the Atlantic railway corridor for that approved branch, where we also want Galicia to be able to intervene and decide. As you know, the Atlantic Axis body has just drawn up a study of the interventions that would require the infrastructure to adapt to the requirements of the European Union. The study figures this intervention in approximately EUR 4 billion. It's an initial approach. But it must be the central government who has been working since this proposal. It's not normal for the central government to have the slightest proposal and it has to be other bodies that have to take steps.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u224	Finish, please.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-12-09-DSPG015.u225	Finally — I have already finished — it is necessary, as I say, also to bet on the intermodality of the corridor by prioritising rail connections to ports and logistics platforms. Ladies and gentlemen of the PSdEG, honestly, you left Galicia once more out of the budgets. You collaborate with this growing spatial inequality that is taking place within the context of the state. Let us hope that today, in this Parliament, in this House, we have a unique voice in which we demand that the Central Government no longer delay this, that we may request these European funds and that they act once and for all the Atlantic corridor. Thank you very much.
